At 298.15 K eq [16J yields ~H~O = 94.0 kJ mol- l • This value compares
favorably with Bottger's quoted value (48) of 100.4 kJ mol- l and his experi-
mental value of 87.5 kJ/mol, Jaenicke's value (28) of 93.1 kJ/mol, and
Kirschner's value of 89.1 kJ/mol calcd for the temp range of 291-298 K (31).
Joannis' calorimetric determination of ~H~O = 93.89 kJ/mol at 286.7K is in
excellent agreement with the value of 94.0 kJ/mol calcd from eq [16J for
this temperature. Since ~H~O is a sensitive test for the accuracy of the
K~O determinations, the evaluator recommends its use as a guide to the
accuracy of K~O data reported as a function of temperature. The work of
Dash, Mohanty, and Panda (20) certainly involves an error based upon this
criteria as discussed above. Klein's data (34) are difficult to interpret
since the ionic strength of the solutions are not known: i.e. his value of
82.1 kJ mol- l might be correct for ~HsO (i.e. at some finite ionic strength)
but is quite low for a ~H~O value.
3. Stability and formation constants at 298.15K.    Inspection of Table 1
indicates acceptable (i.e. within experimental error) agreement for the K~2'
K~3' K0 4 values reported by three different investigators (14, 16, 21),
and mo~erate agreement between K~l values (16,21). Thus despite the large
standard deviations in these formation constants, it seems reasonable to
propose a set of recommended values. These were arrived at by averaging
those K~n's for n = 1-3 obtained by Leden and Nilsson (16) and by Woolley
 (21). Cave and Hume's data were not used in the averaging computations
simply because they do not report any errors in CAg and Ksn: their inclu-
sion would not produce any significant change in computed CAg values. The
tentative    value for K0 4 was taken from Leden and Nilsson (16). Based on
the calculations presenfed below, it is clear that the recommended set of
Ksn for n = 1-4 are not sufficient to describe CAg in solutions of excess
SCN-. Leden and Nilsson (16) and Guynner and Yakhkind (18~ find evidence
for polynuclear species. The latter evidence for Ag3{SCN)6- is weak since
other important soluble species (Ag(SCN)j- and Ag(SCN)~-) were ignored.
Leden and Nilsson have determined a Ks 26 value in a 4 mol dm- 3 medium, and
for lack of any other data, this value for Ks 26 is given along with the
recommended values below: it must however be regarded as highly provisional.
Because of the apparent weaknesses in the calculations of Ksn values at
293K, the evaluator cannot suggest recommended values, nor does he feel that

Equations [32] and [33]were solved by the Newton-Raphson method, and CAg
calculated from eq [26] or [34]. The results are given in Table 3. The
data in this table are of interest for several reasons. First they indicatE
that for all c values considered, the contributions by mononuclear complexe~
to CAg is of prime importance: the contribution by Ag{SCN)2 drops off
rapidly for c > 0.1 mol dm- 3 which explains why this complex has escaped
detection in several investigations. The polynuclear complex makes import-
ant contributions to CAg for values of c ~ 0.1 mol dm- 3 . The fact that
all complexes, except perhaps Ag{SCN)i, exist in nearly equal concentrations
for c values commonly employed in most investigations adds to the difficult~
in the evaluation of the formation constants. The additional fact that
Cave and Hume (l4) report formation constants for mononuclear complexes in
a concentration region where the polynuclear species exists in significant
concentrations is another reason their values were not used in the recom-
mended average values for Ksn •

COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIONAL DATA:
Neglecting the possible formation of Ag(SCN)2 and Ag(SCN·CN)
                                                                  - , the equil-
ibrium constant for the above reaction, KIll' is given by

    KIll = [Ag(CN);]/[SCN-] = K (AgAg(CN)2)/K sO (AgSCN)                   [1]
                               sO
KIll is calcd directly from the data in the above table. Lucas' original
intent was to calculate KsO(Ag Ag(CN)2) from eq [1] using previously deter-
mined values for KsO(AgSCN).  However, the only data at 25 0 C available to
Lucas were those of Kuster and Thiel (1), KsO = 1.17 x 10- 12 , and Abegg
and Cox (2), KsO =1.56 x 10- 12 . To determine the correct value of
KsO(AgSCN), Lucas used the mean value of Ks O(AgAg(CN)2) = 5.01 x 10- 12 in
eq [1] with the experimental KIll values. The latter soly product was
determined by Lucas in the same paper compiled elsewhere in this volume)
from studies on the exchange reactions
                         ....
    AgAg(CN)2(s) + KCl + KAg(CN)2 + AgCl(s)                                [2]
                         ....                                              [3]
    AgAg(CN)2(s) + KBr + KAg(CN)2 + AgBr(s)
                                                    -12
Lucas' average value of KsO(AgSCN) = 1.64 x 10     is in good agreement with
the value of 1.56 x 10- 12 found by Abegg and Cox.

Lucas then uses Abegg and Cox's value of KsO(AgSCN) and his experimental
KIll values (see below) to calculate Ks O(AgAg(CN)2) values. Thus the
reader will find another compilation for Ks O(AgAg(CN)2) which uses the
same data as given in the above data table.

As an example of how Lucas calculated the KIll values, consider the first
data point in the above data table:

    KIll = 0.0827/(0.1093 - 0.0827) = 3.109

For uninegative ions, KIll should be independent of ionic strength since
from eq [1], it is seen that mean activity coefficients should cancel.
Thus the KsO(AgSCN) value obtained from eq [1] will in fact be the standard
value, K~O' depending upon whether the corresponding Ks O(AgAg(CN)2) is a
standard value or the value at a specific ionic strength.    In com~uting
this soly product from eqs. [21 and [3], Lucas used the KsO(AgCl) and
KsO(AgBr) values of 2.0 x 10- 10 (3) and 6.4 x 10- 13 (3), respectively.
The data from references 1 and 3 involve several assumptions concerning
liquid junctions and the degree of dissociation, and are not corrected
for activity effects.  In the compilations for AgCN based on equations [2]
and [3], the compiler has used more recent data for K~O values for AgCl
and AgBr, and has computed the following average value based on Lucas'
data:

          K~O(Ag Ag(CN)2) = (4.3±0.4) x 10- 12 mo1 2 dm- 6
Using this value in eq [1] with Lucas' average KIll = 3.07±0.06, the
compiler arrives at the value

          K~O(AgSCN) = (1.4±0.8) x 10- 12 mo1 2 dm- 6

          C
           Ag
                = (K~0)1/2 = (l.2±0.4) x 10- 6 mol dm- 3

COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIONAL DATA:

At room temp (18-22 o C) the conductivity of the satd solns was constant
within 1-2 minutes after vigorous shaking demonstrating that equilibrium
was rapidly attained. However at 1000C the conductivity slowly increased
linearly with time, and was attributed to the slow dissolution of impuri-
ties. Bottger therefore made two     K(AgSCN) measurements for the first
run after 95 and 125 minutes at lOO.loC (K = 14.76 and 15.18 x 10- 6 ohm- l
cm- l , respectively): the rate of increase is thus 0.84 x 10- 6 ohm- l cm-l/h.
Using this value to correct all four experimental runs, the compiler
computes an average value of K(AgSCN) = (13.79±0.30) x 10- 6 ohm- l em-I.
K(H20) was determined by first making measurements at 460C and 1000C: the
ratio of these two measurements was 1.88±0.08. Before each run, Bottger
measured K(H20) at 460C and multiplied this value by the ratio 1.9 to
obtain the value of K(H20) at 100°C for each of the four experimental
runs.

To correct the specific conductivity of AgSCN to 1000C Bottger estimated
the temp coefficient by adding the temp coeff of AgN03 (0.88%) at 1000C
obtained by A.C. Melcher (no reference was given) with the value (dS/S)dT
calcd from


              (dS/S)dT= ~Hsoln/2RT2 = 0.040                          [3]

where S is the soly and ~Hsoln = 24,000 cal (3). Thus the temp coefficient
is 0.04 + 0.009 = 0.049, and the final value for K(AgSCN) at 100.00C is
13.7 x 10- 6 ohm- l em-I. Bottger gives a value of 13.9 x 10- 6 which is
probably due to a rounding-off error: the compiler can duplicate the K
values for AgCl and AgBr from Bottger's data so that the above difference
is not due to an error in calcn by the compiler.

 To obtain the equivalent conductivity of AgSCN at 100.00C Bottger assumes
that the ratio (Ao(AgN03) - Ao(AgSCN))/Ao(AgN03) is twice as large at 20°C
than it is at 100°C: at 20°C this ratio = 4.6%, and at 1000C it equals
2.3%. Using Melcher's value (no reference) Ao(AgN03) = 367 at 100°C,
Bottger obtains Ao(AgSCN) = 359 ohm- l cm 2 /equiv at 100°C.

Combining the solubility of AgSCN at 1000C with the value obtained at
20 0 C(2) Bottger, using the integrated form of eq [3], calculates a heat of
solution, ~Hsoln' = 20.92 kcal/mol which is in agreement with the value
obtained by Tomsen (3).

COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIO~AL DATA:

The data support the existence of three double salts 1:1:2, 1:2:2, and
1:3:2. No congruent point occurs on the solubility curve of any of these
double salts. These double salts have refractive indices greater than
1.65, are anisotropic and biaxial with optical properties that suggest
they are monoclinic. The compiler has converted the original data to
molal units which are presented below.

COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIONAL DATA:

The solubility product of AgSCN at 2S o C is obtained from the equation

                      log K~O(AgSCN) =      (E~(Ag,AgSCN) - E~(Ag~Ag+»/0.OS9l6                 [8]
Taking ~(Ag,Ag+) = 0.799lV at 2S o C (6) and Pearce and Smith's value for
Eg(Ag,AgSCN) gives the first entry in the data table above. Cave and Hume
 (1) pointed out that the ES value used by Pearce and Smith is incorrect
since it is actually ES: correcting for the liq junction potential (Ej =
1.8mV as calcd from the Henderson equation), we have ES = ES - Ej = 0.3356
- 0.0018 = 0.3338V.      Introducing this result into eq [6] and taking the
mean molal activity coefficient for O.lm KSCN equal to 0.769 (7), it is
found that ~(Ag,AgSCN) = 0.092lV. Using eq [8] gives K~O(AgSCN)= 1.12
x 10- 12 mo1 2 /kg 2 . Cave and Hume report a value of 1.13 x 10- 12 which was
obtained by correcting the former value for the activity of water in O.lm
KSCN soln (Robinson and Stokes give aW = 0.997): i.e. according to Cave and
Hume, Ag + exists as the tetrahydrate Ag+(H20)4, and
           o                       -12 4

COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIONAL DATA:

The relative turbidity of the various solutions was determined after 0.5,
1, 2, 4, 5, 10, 20, and 60 minutes. There was a problem of selecting a
time for which the extrapolated turbidity would have the value of zero.
At larger concentrations (values not specified) of the reacting species,
different times tended to give the same extrapolated value. However this
was not always the case as different times gave different extrapolated
values for the low concentrations. For the cases where extrapolated
values for different times did not agree, the authors' selected a time
at which the particles of the precipitate were fully grown, but had not
started to settle.

COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIONAL DATA:

The intercept with its standard deviation was redetermined by the compiler:
E8bs = 0.15005+0.00006 V. This value leads to a standard deviation of ~
0.25% in the soly product which is meaningless since the magnitude of other
sources of error are unknown (e.g. in temp, liq junction potential, and
measured e.m.f.).

Several problems appear to be present in the determination of the EO value:
EO is calculated from eq [lJ using Ehcal = 0.2378 V. K~O is then calculated
from eq [2J.

However, the calcd K~O still contains an error since the authors use a value
of EO A + = 0.7922V at 30 0 c whereas the compiler calculates a value of
0.79~~V ~2) at this temperature. Using the authors' values it is found that
EO = 0.0878 V and from eq [2J, KsO = 1.94 x 10- 12 . However the authors
report KsO = 1.4 x 10- 12 (as do teritiary sources such as Seidell (3) ).
This difference is evidently due to a juxtaposition error since the latter
KsO value results from taking EO = 0.0788V. Taking E~g Ag+ = 0.7942V at
30 o C, the compiler computes the following value:      '


                   K~O(AgSCN)   = 1.82 x 10- 12 mo1 2 dm- 6

 COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIONAL DATA:

 Each value for KsO in the data table represents a mean of 4 to 13 values.
 For ~ = 0.113 mol drn- 3 there were 18 independent measurements, but 5 were
 excluded from the mean because of irreproducible e.m.f. values which ap-
 parently occurs for Ag+ and SCN- concentrations less than 0.001 mol drn- 3 .
 Similarly 6 measurements are reported for ~ = 0.00113 mol drn- 3 , but only 4
 are used to calc the mean KsO' Extrapolation of the three mean KsO values
 to zero ionic strength gives K~O and the activit¥ coefficients for each
 experimental solution can be calculated from eq ll] below. The authors
 estimate the error in KgO by taking the difference intercepts obtained from
 the experimental plot of logKsO against ~1/2/(1 + ~1/2), and the intercept
 obtained by drawing a straight line from the point log KsO = -11.948 to ~ =
 o and a slope given by the Debye-Hlickel limiting law. A more accurate
 estimation of the error in K~O from standard methods incorporates the sum
 of the variances of each experimental point: the error thus calculated by
 the compiler is 3-4%.

 COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIONAL DATA:

 The solid phase in equilibrium with solns where [SCN-] > 0.5M is NaAg(SCN)2
 2H20: below this concentration NaAg(SCN)2·2H20 is not stable and the solid
 phase is AgSCN. While the authors did analyse this soLid, they could not
 analyse for the water of crystallization: they assume the stoichiometry of
 2 waters of crystallization based on the work of Occleshaw (1). The soly
 product defined in eq [1] is valid only for a constant value of [Na+] = 4.0
 mol/dm 3 • The concentration of SCN- reqUired for use in eq [3] was calcula-
 ted from the measured solUbility of NaAg(SCN)2·2H20 and the distribution of
 thiocyanatoargentate complexes in the saturated solutions (no details are
 given, but presumably are identical to those discussed in a later paper
 (2)   ).

COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIONAL DATA:

The difficulty in the determination of CAg in the absence of NaSCN is
probably due to the adsorption of 110Ag on the walls of the vessel. The
final CAg values were obtained by extrapolation to zero time from a plot of
log CAg against time. For those cases where only one experimental point is
available, the standard deviation was assumed to equal the difference
between the experimental value and the value calculated from the equation




where Sl 0 = 1. Using the soly products determined previously (2), the
stability constants were evaluated graphically (the paper also reports
data obtained potentiometrically in unsaturated solns which yield S values
slightly different from those obtained from the soly method which are listed
in Table 4 below). The concentration of free SCN- was calculated from the
total concentration from

CRITICAL EVALUATION:
 The soly of AgSCN in H20/(CH3)2CO mixtures has been reported by Kratohvil
 and Tezak (1), Woolley (2), and Szarvas, Korondan, and Szab6 (3). A single
 study in pure acetone has also been reported (4). Because of differing
 exptl conditions such as temperature, solvent composition, and ionic
 strength, comparisons are difficult to make, and tentative or recommended
 values cannot be given. One can however make qualitative comparisons to
 determine if any data are grossly inconsistent.

Kratohvil and Tezak studied the soly of AgSCN in two mixed solvents con-
taining 48.5% and 82% acetone by weight: their experiments, based on the
turbidimetric method, were carried out at 293 K in solns of varying ionic
strength. Woolley used a combined radioassay and e.m.f. method to calcu-
late K~O and stabil~ty constants as a function of solvent composition at
298 K.   Both studies re~ort the existence of Ag(SCN)2' but Woolley's work
indicates that Ag(SCN)3 becomes insignificant in solvents containing more
than 34% acetone by weight.   Szarvas et. al. used the potentiometric titrn
method to calc KsO in 0.001 mol dm- 3 at 298 K: however solvent compositions
are reported in terms of vol % which are not defined, and hence cannot be
converted to a weight basis.   In pure acetone, Golub (4) reports a value
for Ks 2 obtained from direct measurements of the soly, CAg. The complex
Ag(SCN)~- was not detected.

 The only KsO data available are from references (2) and (3) at 298 K.
 Little significance can be given to the attempt (1) to calc KsO at 293 K
 from the empirical Ricci-Davis equation (5) as discussed in the compilatio~
 Bg visual observation of the data tables, it is apparent that Woolley's
 KsO values differ significantly from those of Szarvas et. al. The fact
 that the latter KsO values are much lower than Woolley's values may be due
 to the neglect of complex ion formation, or that the silicone membrane
 used in construction of the specific ion electrode is attacked by acetone.

 Ks 2 can be used to make qualitative comparisons since, to a large extent,
 it is independent of concn: i.e. Ks 2 ~ K~2 as seen from the relation


                                                                                          [lJ

 The evaluator found that Woolley's data can be fit by least squares to


    log K~2 = 0.035(wt % acetone) - 3.92              (0    =±0.08)

The correlation coeff for this fit is 0.992, and 0 was calcd from differ-
ences in the obsd and calcd log K~~. Equation [2J was used to calculate
log Ks 2 for several solvent compos~tions as well as for the pure solvents,
and the results are given in the table below. Although the temperatures
differ for several of the entries to the table, it is apparent that quali-
tatiye agreement exists for Ks 2 between the works referenced in 1, 2, and ~

 COHPONENTS:                               EVALUATOR:
     (1) Silver thiocyanate; AgSCN;         Mark Salomon, u.S. Army Electronics
         [1701-93-5]
                                            Command, Fort Monmouth, NJ, U.S.A.
     (2) Dimethyl sulfoxide; C H 0S;
         [67-68-5]            2 6

  (3) Water; H2 0;      [7732-18-5]
 CRITICAL EVALUATION:
   The solubility of AgSCN in mixtures of water and dimethyl sulfoxide has
   been studied at 298 K in two laboratories (1,2). Both studies employed a
   potentiometric titration method to calculate solubility products (1,2)
   and stability constants (1). Courtot-Coupez and Madec (1) report both
   KsO and 62 values as a function of mole fraction of dimethyl sulfoxide:
   the ionic strength of all solutions was maintained at about 0.11 mol dm- 3 •
   Szarvas, Korond~n, and Szabo (2) report only KsO as a function of the
   volume % of dimethyl sulfoxide: they employed constant ionic strength
   solutions of about 0.001 mol dm- 3 •

   Visual inspection of both sets of data shows significant disagreement in
   the log KsO values. Direct comparisons cannot be made since Szarvas et. aL
   report their solvent composition in the poorly defined units of volume %.
   If we assume their 80 vol % (CH3)2S0 mixture was prepared by mixing 20
   volumes of water with 80 volumes of (CH3)2S0, the evaluator calculates a
   mole fraction of 0.503 for dimethyl sulfoxide at 298 K (density of (CH3)2S0
   at 298.15 K is 1.0958 kg m- 3 (3) ). The value of log KsO for this solvent
   composition is -11.34 which differs from the corresponding value of about
   -8.5 which was interpolated from the data of Courtot-Coupez and Madec.
   This difference is much too large to be attributed to differences in ionic
   strength, or the neglect of complex ions by Szarvas et. al. The KsO values
   of Szarvas et. al. for most other water-organic solvent mixtures appear to
   be too low as seen in the compilations and critical evaluations found
   elsewhere in this volume. It would appear that these results are effected
   by some systematic error which is difficult to trace.   It has been suggest-
   ed in another evaluation that it is possible that the silicone rubber
   membrane used in the specific ion electrode is attacked by the organic
   solvent.

CRITICAL EVALUATION:
 The three studies available (1-3) are not directly comparable as seen in
 the following descriptions. Kratohvil and Tezak (1) report CAg as a
 function of [KSCN] at 293 K in a single mixed solvent containing 57.5 %
 p-dioxane by weight. Woolley reports K~O' S~, S~, and S~ values at 298.15
 K for a series of mixed solvents, and the CAg dependence on [SCN-] is shown
 in graphical form. From Woolley's plot of log CAg against log [SCN-], the
 evaluator interpolated (visually) log CAg values for a constant ~CN-] =
 1 x 10-3mo l dm- 3 : the resulting log CA values were p~otted against the
 weight % of p-dioxane.     Interpolating fo a solvent composition of 57.5 %
 p-dioxane, it is found that CAg = 3 x 10- 5 mol dm- 3 • Kratohvil and Tezak
 found that CAg = 4 x 10-5mo l dm- 3 at 293 K for the same solvent composition
 a~ ~~~.

 Szarvas et. al. (3) reported KsO values as a function of volume % p-dioxan~
 and precisely how these compositions are defined is not known.  In addition
 to this uncertainty, the calculated values of KsO appear to be much too
 small. This may represent an error in this work which could be attributed
 to the neglect of the formation of complex ions. The results of Kratohvil
 and Tezak, and of Woolley, confirm the existence of complex species. An
 additional source of error in this latter work (3) may be attributed to the
 attack on the silicone rubber matrix in the specific ion electrode by the
 p-dioxane.

CRITICAL EVALUATION:
The solubility of AgSCN in excess SCN- solns has been studied by direct
measurements over a wide range of H20/C2H50H compositions by Kratohvil and
Tezak at 293 K (1), and by Woolley at 298 K (2). Golub (3) reports a single
point for CAg in pure ethanol containing 0.5326 mol dm- 3 KSCN at 293 K.
Szarvas et. al. (4) used an e.m.f. method to evaluate KsO values at 298 K in
a series of H20/C2HSOH mixtures, and at a constant ionic strength of ~ 0.001
mol dm- 3 • Macfarlane and Hartley (5) are the only investigators to report
a value of K~O in pure ethanol: this value was obtained for 298.2 K from
standard half-cell potentials.

~ratohvil and Tezak used a turbidimetric method to determine CAg at 293 K
~n solutions containing 0.001 ~  KSCN ~ 0.9 mol dm- 3 • Solvent compositions
ranged from 30-90 wt % ethanol. These authors calcd Ks 2 and Ks 3 as averages
based on their data for CAg in solutions of varying ionic strength.
Woolley's Ksn values at 298 K are considerably smaller for equivalent sol-
Vent compositions: this tends to indicate some inconsistancy in one of the
studies. Kratohvil and Tezak's results for Sn (calcd fromS n = KsO/K sn ) are
SUbject to an error of unknown magnitude since the KsO values were calcd
from an empirical relation (6) which was concluded by this evaluator to be
in error (see the evaluations and compilations for the AgSCN/CH30H system).
The CAg data were reproducible to within ±3%, and can be assumed to be ac-
curate within this stated precision.

Woolley reports graphical CAg data as a function of the free [ SCNl : the
data have also been fit to a power series by a least squares method, and
Ksn values for n = 0-3 were evaluated.  It appears that Woolley's KsO data
are much too large based on comparisons with the values in H20/C2H50H mix-
tures obtained in (1) and (3), and the value of log K~O = -14.33 in pure
C2HSOH (5).

The KsO values of Szarvas et. al. appear to be decreasing in a satisfactory
manner as the ethanol content is increased, but the data are of little
utility since the compositions of the solvent mixtures were reported in
vague units of volume %.

In spite of the large exptl error estimated by the compiler for the K~o value
obtained by Macfarlane and Hartley, their value of 4.7 x 10- 3 mo1 2 kg- 2 in
pure ethanol at 298.2 K appears to be reasonable. The large exptl error is
understandable since pure ethanol has traditionally been a difficult solvent
to work with.  In addition, the Ag,AgSCN electrode has a history of poor
reproducibility (e.g. see the critical evaluation and compilations for the
aqueous systems).

CRITICAL EVALUATION:
The two experimental studies available (1,2) are not readily comparable
since they involve different temperatures, different solvent compositions,
and different ionic media. Under these conditions, neither tentative or
recommended values can be given.

Kratohv1l and Tezak (1) carefully measured the solubility of AgSCN 1n a
62% CH30H soln (by wt) at 293 K: the solubility was measured in excess KSCN
solut1ons with varying ionic strength. They used a turbidimetric method,
and the1r results are probably reproducible to within ±3%. The solubility
data was used to calculate a value of Ks 2 = 6 x 10- 3 which appears accept-
able, but may be too high when compared to the value of 2 x 10- 3 obtained
by extrapolation of Woolley's 298 K data. The values for 82 and 83 are not
considered reliable since they were calculated using a KsO value which in
turn was calculated from the Ricci-Davis equation (3): as discussed in the
compilation, several significant errors were involved in the use of this
empirical equation.

Woolley calculated K~O and 8g values at 298 K in solutions of varying meth-
anol content.  Ionic concentrations were obtained from e.m.f. measurements,
and the solubility of AgSCN obtained by radiochemical analysis. The data
were f1t by least squares to an equation yielding K~O and standard formation
constants for the mononuclear complexes AgSCN, Ag(SCN)2' and Ag(SCN)~-.

The stability constants calculated by Kratohvil and Tezak are averages
obtained from data points of varying ionic strength. The assumpt10n that
the format1on constants are independent of ionic strength is probably valid
for Ks 2' but not for Ks 3.

COMMENTS AND/OR ADDITIONAL DATA:

Since the dielectric constant of 1,2-dimethoxyethane is small (E = 7.2 D
at 25 0 C), ion-pairing effects are expected to be large •. From the equili-
brium constants given above, the authors derive the following relationships:




                  ,
logB
       2
           = logB 2 + pK G (AgC10 4 ) + 2pK G (Bu 4NSCN) - pK G (Bu 4NC10 4 ) -



where KG is the dissociation constant of the indicated ion-pair. The pK
values for AgC104 and BU4NC104 (3.8 and 5.1, respectively) were obtained
from a previous study (1). pKG values for BU4NSCN and BU4NAg(SCN)2 were
both assumed to equal 3.5 (note: a typographical error in the paper in-
correctly lists pK G = 4.5).

(1) Silver thiocyanate; AgSCN:             Mark Salomon, U.S. Army Electronics

CRITICAL EVALUATION:
Parker and co-workers (1) used potentiometric titration data to evaluate
KsO and 82 in a constant ionic strength medium of 0.05 mol dm- 3 at 298.15 K.
Le Demezet et. ale (2) used a similar experimental technique to determine
KsQ, 82' 83 , 823' and 834 under the following experimental conditions:
the ionic strength was constant at about 0.11 mol dm- 3 , and the temperature,
which was not specified in the paper, was probably around 293 to 295 K.
Parker's value of log KsO = 7.1 at 298 K cannot be compared to the value of
log KsO = 7.6 obtained by Le Demezet et. ale due to the uncertainty in
the temperature of this latter work.
Luehrs and Abate (3) determined the solubility of AgSCN in AgNO~ solutions
and find evidence for the presence of a single polynuclear spec1es:
Ag 2 SCN+. The stability constant for this species, 821,was calculated from
the experimental solubility data and a value of log KsO = -7.3: the authors
did not evaluate this KsO value, nor did they give a numerical value or
quote a source. This value was calculated by the compiler using the solubi-
lity data and the reported values for 821. The experimental temperature
was about 297 K and a constant ionic strength medium of 1 mol dm- 3 was
employed. In the absence of molar activity coefficient data for 1 mol dm- 3
solutions, the significance of this value for KsO is not clear.

CRITICAL EVALUATION:
Buckley and Hartley (1) were the first to evaluate K~O(AgSCN) using an
e.m.f. method in which the standard electrode potent~al for the Ag,AgSCN
half-cell was evaluated. The resulting value at 298.15 K is

  K~O(AgSCN) = 1.8 x 10- 14 mo1 2 kg- 2                , or log     K~O(AgSCN) = -13.7

There are a number of sources of error in the evaluation of this K~O: un-
pUblished data for ion mobilities were used to evaluate liq junction
potentials; e.m.f. values were reported to an accuracy of 0.1 mV when it is
apparent that the reproducibility is no better than tl mV; the mean molal
activity coeff, Yt, for 0.01 m AgN03 is assumed to be identical to that for
0.01 m HCl; similarly Yt for NaCl solns are assumed to be identical to those
for equal concns of HCl. The std deviation in K~O based solely on a repro-
ducibility of tlmV is ~ t4%, and the total error due to the remaining
sources of uncertainty is probably at least tlO%.

Parker and co-workers (2) determined K~O at 298.15 K in a constant ionic
strength medium of 0.01 mol drn- 3 • The~r result is

                               4         2
   KSO(AgSCN) = 1.3 x 10-          mo1       dm- 6 ,     or   log KsO(AgSCN) = -13.9

Correcting for activity effects using the Davies equation (3)

      log Yt    = _A~1/2/(1 + ~1/2) + 0.3A~ = -0.167
The evaluator obtains the following result for the stnd equil constant:

              log K~O   = log KsO + 2log Yt = -14.2
Converting this stnd value from a volume to a weight basis,


   log(K~0/mo12kg-2) = log(K~0/mo12dm-6) - 2log do = -14.0
In these calculations, the Debye-Huckel A factor (1.895 mol- l / 2dm l • 5 ) and
the density of methanol (do = 0.7866 kg m- 3 ) were taken from reference 4.

Tentative Value of K~O(AgSCN) at 298.15 K

Due to the large experimental errors associated with the two K~O values,
a tentative value appears more appropriate than a recommended one. Taking
the mean KsO's and combining the probable experimental error of tlO% with
the deviation from the mean, we have

                            log(K~0/mo12 kg- 2 ) = -13.9tO.3
                            log(K~0/mo12 dm- 6 ) = -14.ltO.3

COMMENTS AND lOR ADDITIONAL DATA:
Since a number of assumptions are involved in the calcn of the EO,s, a de-
tailed description seems worthwhile. The liq junction potentials, Ej' were
calcd from
  E = (RT/F)ln (AlA')     or E j = {(u-v)/(u+v)}(RT/F) ln (m l /m 2 )
   j
The former equation was used for two monovalent salts having one ion in
common, and possessing equivalent conductivities A and A'. The latter eqn
was used for solns of the same monovalent salt whose concentrations are ml
and m2 and whose cations and anions have mobilities u and v, respectively.
The data for these calculations were obtained from earlier work (3), and
from unpublished data: some of these data are given in the present paper.
Assuming y+ for O.Olm AgC104 = 0.745 (i.e. the experimental value (1) for
HC1), we have

         E~g,Ag+ = E 3 + E 7 + (RT/F)ln(O.Ol x 0.745)                    = 0.7646V

Similarly taking y±= 0.664 for 0.02 m NaSCN,

         E~g,AgSCN = E 5 + E 6 - E 4 + (RT/F)ln(0.02 x 0.664)                   = -0.0489V
The authors attempted to use AgN03 solns in cell [3J but obtained lower
E~ A + values which was attributed to incomplete dissociation of this salt
ingme~hanol. Electrolytically prepared Ag,AgSCN electrodes were neither
reproducible or constant.

 Since the dielectric constant of tetrahydrofuran is small (c =7.4 D at 25 C)
 ion-pairing effects are expected to be large. From the equilibrium
 equations given above, the authors derive the following relationships:




 where KG is the dissociation constant of the indicated ion-pair. The pK G
 values for AgCI04 (unpublished results) and BU4NCI04 (2) are respectively,
 3.4 and 6.2. The pKG values for BU4NSCN and BU4N·Ag(SCN)2 were assumed to
 equal 3.5 (note: a typographical error in the paper incorrectly lists a
 value of 4.5).
